64	\clearpage
65	\section*{Limitations}
66	
67	Our findings are bounded by (1)~model scale ($\leq$7B, with only one 7B configuration tested; the 7B condition uses Qwen2.5-7B-Base rather than Instruct, introducing a base-vs-instruct confound---though $\Kempirical{=}2$ consistency across this variation provides additional robustness evidence; cross-scale scoring rather than self-scoring), (2)~prompt-chain rather than retrain-chain protocols (our $\Kempirical$ is a conservative upper bound on retrain-chain $\Kstar$ under monotonic-degradation assumptions), (3)~English C4 text only, (4)~four decoding strategies, (5)~threshold sensitivity ($\Kempirical$ ranges from 2--3 at $\theta{=}0.55$ to 1 at $\theta{=}0.70$; all nine conditions yield point-estimate $\Kempirical{=}2$ at $\theta{=}0.60$, with 8/9 confirmed by bootstrap CI---one decoding variant has 2v3 CI upper marginally at 0.601), (6)~marginal boundary (Qwen-continuation 1v2 CI lower bound exceeds $\theta$ by only 0.002; cross-scorer validation with both Pythia and OLMo scorers drops this condition to $\Kempirical{=}1$, Section~\ref{sec:transfer}), (7)~the 15D feature set yields a feature-set-specific lower bound ($\Ktheory$ may be strictly higher), (8)~the neural probe boundary is model-dependent ($\Kempirical^{\text{neural}}{=}3$ for Qwen only; $2$ for Pythia/OLMo), likely reflecting self-familiarity rather than a universal signal, (9)~while overall feature rankings are highly consistent across models ($\rho \geq 0.85$), depth-pair-level agreement weakens at the 1v2 boundary ($\tau \approx 0.37$), indicating that per-feature contributions at the critical boundary vary across models, (10)~cross-model scoring reduces $\Kempirical$ from 2 to 1 for continuation-mode data (Table~\ref{tab:crossscorer}), indicating that the 1v2 boundary signal has a model-specific component consistent with the representation-dependent neural probe finding; in deployment the scorer model is known, making self-scoring the natural choice, but unknown-generator scenarios remain a limitation, and (11)~all experiments use 256-token segments matching common web-scale chunking; $\Kempirical$ is defined conditionally on segment length, and longer documents with more stable per-document statistics could yield higher $\Kempirical$.
68	Detailed analysis appears in Appendix~\ref{sec:extended_limitations}.
69	
70	% === Ethics Statement (ACL required, unnumbered) ===
71	\section*{Ethics Statement}
72	
73	Generational depth estimation is intended for data curation and corpus auditing---positive-sum applications that improve training data quality.
```

corpora are increasingly contaminated by recursive synthetic content, yet no method estimates a document's generational depth.\nWe measure the discrimination boundary $\\Kempirical$---the maximum reliably detectable, but the second renders text statistically indistinguishable from deeper synthetic content---a \\emph{one-wash laundering} effect.\nModel-free statistical features enable 80--92\\% cross-model transfer retention among continuation-mode generators with scorer-invariant feature ordering ($\\rho > 0.90$), and cross-scorer validation confirms that $\\Kempirical$ is governed by the generation process rather than the scoring model.\nBoth supervised classification and zero-shot frontier LLMs fail comparably at depths ${\\geq}2$ (recall 14--22\\% vs.\\ 8--38\\%), providing converging evidence consistent with $\\Kempirical{=}2$ reflecting a data-intrinsic boundary rather than a method limitation.\nDownstream, $\\Kempirical{=}2$ correctly predicts that binary curation $2$ for Pythia/OLMo), likely reflecting self-familiarity rather than a universal signal, (9)~while overall feature rankings are highly consistent across models ($\\rho \\geq 0.85$), depth-pair-level agreement weakens at the 1v2 boundary ($\\tau \\approx 0.37$), indicating that per-feature contributions at the critical boundary vary across models, (10)~cross-model scoring reduces $\\Kempirical$ from 2 to 1 for continuation-mode data (Table~\\ref{tab:crossscorer}), indicating that the 1v2 boundary signal has a model-specific component consistent with the representation-dependent neural probe finding; in deployment the scorer model is known, making self-scoring the natural choice, but unknown-generator scenarios remain a limitation, (11)~all experiments use 256-token segments matching common web-scale chunking; $\\Kempirical$ is defined conditionally on segment length, and longer documents with more stable per-document statistics could yield higher $\\Kempirical$, and (12)~structural composition: depth~0 texts are human-authored (mean ${\\approx}$191 tokens after truncation) while depth~1+ are machine-generated (mean 214--256 tokens); this length asymmetry makes 0v1 detection \\emph{more conservative} (shorter texts provide less feature-extraction context), not inflated, and depths 1--5 exhibit no systematic length trend (EOS truncation rates stable across depths within each model), so the critical $\\Kempirical{=}2$ finding---based on 1v2 vs.\\ 2v3 comparisons---is unaffected by this confound.",
          " Detailed analysis appears in Appendix~\\ref{sec:extended_limitations}.",
          " ",
          " % === Ethics Statement (ACL required, unnumbered) ==="
        ]
      }

"In continuation mode, the generation prompt (first half of the predecessor text) is discarded; only the newly generated tokens are retained as the depth-$k$ text. <details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01ViL8x1BHjF1hz4QYasvQ3K`</summary>

```text
34	
35	\subsection{Structural Confound: Token Length and EOS Truncation}
36	\label{sec:length_confound}
37	
38	A potential confound is that EOS truncation at the 256-token generation limit could create systematic length differences across depths, inflating AUC through a length shortcut rather than distributional signatures.
39	We quantify this concern across all model configurations (full per-depth statistics in Table~\ref{tab:token_length}).
40	
41	Depth~0 (human text, truncated from C4) averages ${\sim}$191 tokens across all configurations, while depth~1+ (machine-generated) averages 195--256 tokens depending on model.
42	The length asymmetry thus runs \emph{opposite} to the confound direction: human texts are shorter, so any length-based shortcut would make 0v1 detection \emph{harder} (a classifier exploiting ``shorter$=$machine'' would fail), not easier.
43	
44	Within machine-generated depths 1--5, token counts are remarkably stable.
45	For Qwen-rewrite, depths 1--5 average 252--254 tokens (range: 2 tokens); for Pythia, 214--218 tokens (range: 4 tokens); for Qwen-7B, 193--197 tokens (range: 4 tokens).
46	This stability means the critical $\Kempirical{=}2$ finding---based on 1v2 vs.\ 2v3 comparisons among machine-generated texts---cannot be driven by length differences, since depths 1 through 5 have effectively identical token counts within each model.
47	
48	OLMo-1B provides the strongest control: it generates exactly 256 tokens at all depths 1--5 with $<$1\% EOS truncation (Table~\ref{tab:token_length}), completely eliminating length variation as a possible confound.
49	$\Kempirical{=}2$ holds for OLMo (1v2 $\auc = 0.615$, 2v3 $\auc = 0.550$; Table~\ref{tab:s8_cv_folds}), demonstrating that the depth boundary arises from distributional rather than length-based signals.
50	
51	EOS truncation rates vary across models (Table~\ref{tab:token_length}): Qwen-rewrite and decoding ablations show mild truncation (7--16\% at depth~1+), Qwen-continuation moderate truncation (17--20\%), while Pythia (34--36\%) and Qwen-7B (45--47\%) exhibit higher rates.
52	Crucially, truncation rates are \emph{stable across depths 1--5 within each model}, so EOS effects do not create a depth-correlated length gradient that could confound pairwise discrimination.
53	The models with highest truncation (Pythia, Qwen-7B) still yield $\Kempirical{=}2$ with the same step-function AUC decay as low-truncation models, further ruling out length as a driver of the boundary.
```

noise at near-threshold values.\nCross-scale validation at 7B (Section~\\ref{sec:kstar_boundary}) extends this from 1.5B to 7B despite reduced 0v1 signal ($0.9638$ vs.\\ $> 0.99$).\n\nThe practical consequence is a three-tier taxonomy: human-authored (depth~0), first-generation synthetic (depth~1), and deeper synthetic (depth~$\\geq 2$).\nA single generation pass suffices to render text statistically indistinguishable from multiply recycled content---a \\emph{one-wash laundering} effect that limits depth-aware corpus curation to a binary boundary.\nThis connects to model collapse: Shumailov et al.~\\cite{shumailov2024collapse} observed that tail-distribution degradation is most acute in the earliest generations; our prompt-chain results show an analogous per-text pattern.\n\nPer-depth analysis provides the most direct evidence that this boundary is not methodological.\nOBD achieves 96\\% depth-0 recall but only 14--22\\% for depths 2--4; frontier LLMs show 69--77\\% depth-0 recall and 8--38\\% for depths 2--4 (McNemar $p > 0.05$ vs.\\ OBD; Table~\\ref{tab:s3_llm_zeroshot}).\nThis convergence across fundamentally different approaches---supervised statistical classification and zero-shot frontier LLMs---provides converging evidence that the saturation reflects a data-intrinsic boundary rather than a method limitation.\nBinary detector confidence corroborates: despite moderate Spearman correlations ($|\\rho|$ up to 0.46), 6-class accuracy is at or below chance ($\\leq 0.199$ vs.\\ 0.167 baseline), and Qwen-rewrite exhibits a sign reversal ($\\rho > 0$), disqualifying confidence as a reliable depth proxy.\n\n\\subsection{Why Graduated Filtering Fails}\n\\label{sec:negative_graduated}\n\nSince 2v3 $\\auc < \\theta$ for all configurations, depth assignments for $d \\geq 2$ approximate random labels; graduated weighting injects noise into importance weights, explaining the monotonic improvement with $\\alpha$ as it converges toward binary thresholding (Appendix~\\ref{sec:extended_limitations}).\nDSIR fails for a different reason---bigram feature hashing captures domain shift but not depth-correlated distributional signatures.\n\n\\subsection{Representation Dependence of the Boundary}\n\\label{sec:repr_dependence}\n\n$\\Kempirical$ is representation-dependent: frozen Qwen embeddings yield $\\Kempirical^{\\text{neural}} = 3$, while Pythia and OLMo match the 15D boundary at $\\Kempirical^{\\text{neural}} = 2$ (Table~\\ref{tab:neural_probe_crossmodel}).\nThe hierarchy $\\Kempirical^{15\\text{D}}{=}2 < \\Kempirical^{\\text{neural}} \\in \\{2,3\\}$ reflects an interpretability--sensitivity tradeoff: learned representations recover additional depth signal sufficient to push $\\Kempirical$ to 3 for one model (Qwen), but this gain is model-dependent---the 15D features trade one depth level of sensitivity for cross-model universality (9/9 conditions $\\Kempirical{=}2$, 3/3 models identical), making them the preferred operational choice.\nCross-scorer validation reinforces this: alternative scorers preserve feature rankings ($\\rho > 0.90$) but reduce the marginal Qwen-continuation boundary from $\\Kempirical = 2$ to 1, while rewrite-mode $\\Kempirical = 2$ is preserved---$\\Kempirical$ is governed by the generation operator rather than the scoring model.\n\n\\subsection{Structural Confound: Token Length and EOS Truncation}\n\\label{sec:length_confound}\n\nA potential confound is that EOS truncation at the 256-token generation limit could create systematic length differences across depths, inflating AUC through a length shortcut rather than distributional signatures.\nWe quantify this concern across all model configurations (full per-depth statistics in Table~\\ref{tab:token_length}).\n\nDepth~0 (human text, truncated from C4) averages ${\\sim}$191 tokens across all configurations, while depth~1+ (machine-generated) averages 195--256 tokens depending on model.\nThe length asymmetry thus runs \\emph{opposite} to the confound direction: human texts are shorter, so any length-based shortcut would make 0v1 detection \\emph{harder} (a classifier exploiting ``shorter$=$machine'' would fail), not easier.\n\nIn continuation mode, the generation prompt (first half of the predecessor text) is discarded; only the newly generated tokens are retained as the depth-$k$ text.\nConsequently, all texts at depths $\\geq 1$ consist entirely of machine-generated content, and the 1v2 boundary compares texts that differ only in their conditioning context (human-authored vs.\\ machine-generated prompt), not in their structural composition.\n\nWithin machine-generated depths 1--5, token counts are remarkably stable.\nFor Qwen-rewrite, depths 1--5 average 252--254 tokens (range: 2 tokens); for Pythia, 214--218 tokens (range: 4 tokens); for Qwen-7B, 193--197 tokens (range: 4 tokens).\nThis stability means the critical $\\Kempirical{=}2$ finding---based on 1v2 vs.\\ 2v3 comparisons among machine-generated texts---cannot be driven by length differences, since depths 1 through 5 have effectively identical token counts within each model.\n\nOLMo-1B provides the strongest control: it generates exactly 256 tokens at all depths 1--5 with $<$1\\% EOS truncation (Table~\\ref{tab:token_length}), completely eliminating length variation as a possible confound.\n$\\Kempirical{=}2$ holds for OLMo (1v2 $\\auc = 0.615$, 2v3 $\\auc = 0.550$; Table~\\ref{tab:s8_cv_folds}), demonstrating that the depth boundary arises from distributional rather than length-based signals.\n\nEOS truncation rates vary across models (Table~\\ref{tab:token_length}): Qwen-rewrite and decoding ablations show mild truncation (7--16\\% at depth~1+), Qwen-continuation moderate truncation (17--20\\%), while Pythia (34--36\\%) and Qwen-7B (45--47\\%) exhibit higher rates.\nCrucially, truncation rates are \\emph{stable across depths 1--5 within each model}, so EOS effects do not create a depth-correlated length gradient that could confound pairwise discrimination.\nThe models with highest truncation (Pythia, Qwen-7B) still yield $\\Kempirical{=}2$ with the same step-function AUC decay as low-truncation models, further ruling out length as a driver of the boundary.\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Extended Discussion}\n\\label{sec:extended_discussion}\n\n\\subsection{Theoretical Implications of $\\Kempirical = 2$}\n\nThe DPI guarantees a finite $\\Ktheory$; our result establishes $\\Kempirical = 2$ across all 9 conditions (1B--7B), aligning with model collapse convergence~\\cite{shumailov2024collapse, dohmatob2024model, alemohammad2024selfconsuming}.\nDecoding strategy modestly affects signal strength (1v2 $\\auc \\in [0.6866, 0.7039]$) but does not shift the boundary.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Transfer Implications}\n\nCross-model classification retains 80--92\\% accuracy with scorer-invariant feature rankings ($\\rho > 0.90$), enabling single-classifier deployment across generators.\nAbsolute discriminability at the $\\Kempirical$ boundary is scorer-sensitive: alternative scorers reduce Qwen-continuation $\\Kempirical$ from 2 to 1, while rewrite-mode $\\Kempirical = 2$ is preserved.\nModel family similarity modulates transfer (Pythia$\\leftrightarrow$OLMo: 90--92\\% vs.\\ Qwen-cont$\\leftrightarrow$OLMo: 80--85\\%).\n\n\\begin{minipage}{\\columnwidth}\n\\centering\n\\captionof{table}{Cross-scorer validation on Qwen-generated text. Alternative scorers (Pythia-1.4B, OLMo-1B) preserve feature ranking ($\\rho > 0.90$) and rewrite-mode $\\Kempirical = 2$, but reduce continuation-mode $\\Kempirical$ from 2 to 1.}\n\\label{tab:crossscorer}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\\input{tables/tab_crossscorer}}\n\\end{minipage}\n\n\\subsection{Feature Importance Across Decoding Strategies}\n\nFeature importance ordering varies across decoding strategies: greedy and $T{=}0.7$ share highly correlated rankings ($\\rho = 0.914$), while greedy vs.\\ $T{=}1.2$ yields lower correlation ($\\rho = 0.53$, nonsignificant after Bonferroni).\nThe depth boundary is robust but the optimal feature subset is strategy-dependent.\n\n\\subsection{Scale and Neural Probe}\n\\label{sec:extended_neural_probe}\n\nCross-scale validation confirms $\\Kempirical = 2$ at 7B (1v2 $\\auc = 0.6168$ $[0.608, 0.626]$, 2v3 $\\auc = 0.5492$; Table~\\ref{tab:kstar_ci}); the 7B 0v1 $\\auc$ (0.9638) is ${\\sim}$3\\,pp lower than ${\\leq}$1.5B models, consistent with larger models generating more human-like text. Scaling beyond 7B remains open.\n\nThe neural probe yields model-dependent $\\Kempirical^{\\text{neural}}$: $3$ for Qwen (self-familiarity effect from instruction-tuned representations) but $2$ for Pythia and OLMo, matching the 15D boundary.\nThe 15D model-free features yield $\\Kempirical = 2$ universally, confirming their role as a robust lower bound across architectures.\n\n\\subsection{LLM Zero-Shot Baseline}\n\\label{sec:extended_llm}\n\nGPT-5.5 achieves 33.2\\% accuracy (MAE = 1.10) and Claude Opus~4.6 33.1\\% (MAE = 1.15) on a balanced 500-sample test set, comparable to OBD-LightGBM's 38.0\\% (95\\% CI: $[0.340, 0.422]$; McNemar $p = 0.081$, $0.069$; Table~\\ref{tab:s3_llm_zeroshot}).\nPer-depth recall confirms convergent failure at depths $\\geq 2$ (Section~\\ref{sec:why_kstar}).\nWhen 6-class probabilities are marginalized to 5 classes ($P(d{\\geq}4)$), accuracy rises to 44.4\\% but concentrates in depth-4 (recall: 22\\%$\\to$80\\%) while depths 2--3 collapse to 2--8\\%; we report raw 6-class results.\n\n\\subsection{Practical Considerations}\n\nAll filtering strategies degrade general capabilities (MMLU: 0.6018 $\\to$ 0.5865--0.5941; HellaSwag: 0.6821 $\\to$ 0.6638--0.6701); our 100\\% synthetic setting is an upper bound---in mixed corpora, degradation scales with contamination fraction~\\cite{shumailov2024collapse, dohmatob2024model}.\n$\\Kstar$-aware binary filtering reduces MMLU degradation by ${\\sim}$50\\% ($-$1.53\\,pp $\\to$ $-$0.77\\,pp), requires no per-model calibration, and is combinable with distribution-matching methods (DSIR, DoReMi).\n\n\\subsection{Extended Limitations}\n\\label{sec:extended_limitations}\n\n\\emph{Prompt-chain versus retrain-chain.}\nUnder monotonic-degradation assumptions~\\cite{shumailov2024collapse}, $\\Kstar^{\\text{retrain}} \\leq \\Kstar^{\\text{prompt}}$, making $\\Kempirical = 2$ a conservative estimate.\n\n\\emph{Threshold sensitivity.}\nAt $\\theta = 0.55$, $\\Kempirical = 3$ for Qwen-rewrite/continuation; at $\\theta = 0.70$, $\\Kempirical = 1$ universally (Figure~\\ref{fig:kstar_theta}).\nQwen-continuation's 1v2 CI lower bound exceeds $\\theta$ by only 0.2\\,pp; all ten boundary comparisons yield permutation $p < 0.001$.\n\n\\emph{Structural confound: token length.}\nDepth~0 texts average ${\\sim}$191 subword tokens (C4 truncation), while depth~1+ texts average 195--256 tokens depending on model (Table~\\ref{tab:token_length}).\nThis asymmetry is directionally opposite to a length-inflation confound: shorter depth-0 texts provide less feature-extraction context, making 0v1 discrimination harder, not easier.\nWithin depths 1--5, mean token counts are stable (within-model range $\\leq$ 4 tokens for all configurations except Qwen-continuation at 6 tokens), so the pairwise comparisons underlying $\\Kempirical{=}2$ are unaffected by length.\nEOS truncation rates are similarly depth-stable within each model: Qwen-rewrite 7--11\\%, Pythia 34--36\\%, OLMo $<$1\\%, Qwen-continuation 17--20\\%, Qwen-7B 45--47\\%.\nModels with the highest truncation (Pythia, Qwen-7B) still yield $\\Kempirical{=}2$ with the same step-function AUC decay pattern as low-truncation models (Table~\\ref{tab:kstar_ci}), confirming that EOS truncation does not drive the boundary.\n\n\\emph{Graduated filtering.}\nAll $\\alpha$ values (0.1--0.9) underperform binary-ours on MMLU ($\\alpha{=}0.1$: 0.5873; $\\alpha{=}0.9$: 0.5892 vs.\\ 0.5941); at $\\alpha{=}0.9$, HellaSwag matches exactly (0.6701).\nBinary-ours significantly outperforms all alternatives on MMLU ($p_{\\text{corr}} \\leq 0.021$); Graduated$_{0.9}$ is indistinguishable on HellaSwag ($p = 1.00$) but worse on MMLU ($p_{\\text{corr}} = 0.011$).\n\\label{tab:pairwise_significance}\n",

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
The Limitations in main.tex uses "mean 214--256 tokens" which is the range of depth 1+ means across models. Looking at the Director's data:

| Model | depth 1 |
|-------|---------|
| Pythia | 214 |
| OLMo | 256 |
| Qwen-rw | 254 |
| Qwen-cont | 244 |

So the minimum depth 1+ mean across models is Pythia at 214. But looking at ALL depths 1-5, the minimum might be different. The Director's data shows Pythia depth 1 = 214, depth 5 = 218. So 214 is the lowest depth 1+ value.


I notice the discussion.tex now reports "193--256" which includes Qwen-7B's depth 3 value of 193, whereas the Limitations section appears to focus on depth 1 values specifically. I need to check the exact wording in main.tex to clarify what range each section is actually reporting.

Depth Estimation of Recursive Synthetic Text:\\\\Measuring the Discrimination Boundary}\n\n\\author{Anonymous}\n\n\\begin{document}\n\\maketitle\n\n% === Abstract ===\n\\begin{abstract}\nWeb-scale corpora are increasingly contaminated by recursive synthetic content, yet no method estimates a document's generational depth.\nWe measure the discrimination boundary $\\Kempirical$---the maximum depth at which adjacent generations remain distinguishable---using 15 statistical features and an OBD-LightGBM classifier.\nAcross 5 model configurations (1B--7B) and 4 decoding strategies, $\\Kempirical{=}2$ at $\\theta{=}0.60$ (Cohen's $h{\\approx}0.20$), consistent across all 9 tested conditions under self-scoring (most marginal: 0.2\\,pp above $\\theta$): a single generation step is reliably detectable, but the second renders text statistically indistinguishable from deeper synthetic content---a \\emph{one-wash laundering} effect.\nModel-free statistical features enable 80--92\\% cross-model transfer retention among continuation-mode generators with scorer-invariant feature ordering ($\\rho > 0.90$), and cross-scorer validation confirms that $\\Kempirical$ is governed by the generation process rather than the scoring model.\nBoth supervised classification and zero-shot frontier LLMs fail comparably at depths ${\\geq}2$ (recall 14--22\\% vs.\\ 8--38\\%), providing converging evidence consistent with $\\Kempirical{=}2$ reflecting a data-intrinsic boundary rather than a method limitation.\nDownstream, $\\Kempirical{=}2$ correctly predicts that binary curation minimizes degradation most effectively among all tested strategies.\n\\end{abstract}\n\n% === Section 1: Introduction ===\n\\input{introduction}\n\n% === Section 2: Related Work ===\n\\input{related_work}\n\n% === Section 3: Method ===\n\\input{method}\n\n% === Section 4: Experiments ===\n\\input{experiments_c1}\n\n% === Section 5: K*-Aware Data Filtering ===\n\\input{experiments_c3}\n\n% === Section 6: Discussion ===\n\\input{discussion}\n\n% === Section 7: Conclusion ===\n\\input{conclusion}\n\n% === Limitations (ACL required, unnumbered) ===\n\\clearpage\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\nOur findings are bounded by (1)~model scale ($\\leq$7B, with only one 7B configuration tested; the 7B condition uses Qwen2.5-7B-Base rather than Instruct, introducing a base-vs-instruct confound---though $\\Kempirical{=}2$ consistency across this variation provides additional robustness evidence; cross-scale scoring rather than self-scoring), (2)~prompt-chain rather than retrain-chain protocols (our $\\Kempirical$ is a conservative upper bound on retrain-chain $\\Kstar$ under monotonic-degradation assumptions), (3)~English C4 text only, (4)~four decoding strategies, (5)~threshold sensitivity ($\\Kempirical$ ranges from 2--3 at $\\theta{=}0.55$ to 1 at $\\theta{=}0.70$; all nine conditions yield point-estimate $\\Kempirical{=}2$ at $\\theta{=}0.60$, with 8/9 confirmed by bootstrap CI---one decoding variant has 2v3 CI upper marginally at 0.601), (6)~marginal boundary (Qwen-continuation 1v2 CI lower bound exceeds $\\theta$ by only 0.002; cross-scorer validation with both Pythia and OLMo scorers drops this condition to $\\Kempirical{=}1$, Section~\\ref{sec:transfer}), (7)~the 15D feature set yields a feature-set-specific lower bound ($\\Ktheory$ may be strictly higher), (8)~the neural probe boundary is model-dependent ($\\Kempirical^{\\text{neural}}{=}3$ for Qwen only; $2$ for Pythia/OLMo), likely reflecting self-familiarity rather than a universal signal, (9)~while overall feature rankings are highly consistent across models ($\\rho \\geq 0.85$), depth-pair-level agreement weakens at the 1v2 boundary ($\\tau \\approx 0.37$), indicating that per-feature contributions at the critical boundary vary across models, (10)~cross-model scoring reduces $\\Kempirical$ from 2 to 1 for continuation-mode data (Table~\\ref{tab:crossscorer}), indicating that the 1v2 boundary signal has a model-specific component consistent with the representation-dependent neural probe finding; in deployment the scorer model is known, making self-scoring the natural choice, but unknown-generator scenarios remain a limitation, (11)~all experiments use 256-token segments matching common web-scale chunking; $\\Kempirical$ is defined conditionally on segment length, and longer documents with more stable per-document statistics could yield higher $\\Kempirical$, and (12)~structural composition: depth~0 texts are human-authored (mean ${\\approx}$191 subword tokens after truncation) while depth~1+ are machine-generated (mean 195--256 tokens depending on model; Table~\\ref{tab:token_length}); this length asymmetry makes 0v1 detection \\emph{more conservative} (shorter texts provide less feature-extraction context), not inflated, and depths 1--5 exhibit no systematic length trend (within-model range $\\leq$ 6 tokens; EOS truncation rates stable across depths within each model), so the critical $\\Kempirical{=}2$ finding---based on 1v2 vs.\\ 2v3 comparisons---is unaffected by this confound (Section~\\ref{sec:length_confound}).\nDetailed analysis appears in Appendix~\\ref{sec:extended_limitations}.\n\n% === Ethics Statement (ACL required, unnumbered) ===\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nGenerational depth estimation is intended for data curation and corpus auditing---positive-sum applications that improve training data quality.\nOur experiments use the publicly available C4 corpus and do not involve personally identifiable information.\nA potential dual-use concern is that depth estimation techniques could inform adversarial strategies to evade detection (e.g., by calibrating rewriting to mimic earlier-generation statistics); we note that this risk is shared by all detection research and that the binary detection boundary ($\\auc \\geq 0.96$ at 0v1) remains robust.\n\n\\bibliography{references}\n\n\\input{appendix}\n\\input{appendix_extended}\n\n\\end{document}\n",

all nine conditions yield point-estimate $\\Kempirical{=}2$ at $\\theta{=}0.60$, with 8/9 confirmed by bootstrap CI---one decoding variant has 2v3 CI upper marginally at 0.601), (6)~marginal boundary (Qwen-continuation 1v2 CI lower bound exceeds $\\theta$ by only 0.002; cross-scorer validation with both Pythia and OLMo scorers drops this condition to $\\Kempirical{=}1$, Section~\\ref{sec:transfer}), (7)~the 15D feature set yields a feature-set-specific lower bound ($\\Ktheory$ may be strictly higher), (8)~the neural probe boundary is model-dependent ($\\Kempirical^{\\text{neural}}{=}3$ for Qwen only; $2$ for Pythia/OLMo), likely reflecting self-familiarity rather than a universal signal, (9)~while overall feature rankings are highly consistent across models ($\\rho \\geq 0.85$), depth-pair-level agreement weakens at the 1v2 boundary ($\\tau \\approx 0.37$), indicating that per-feature contributions at the critical boundary vary across models, (10)~cross-model scoring reduces $\\Kempirical$ from 2 to 1 for continuation-mode data (Table~\\ref{tab:crossscorer}), indicating that the 1v2 boundary signal has a model-specific component consistent with the representation-dependent neural probe finding; in deployment the scorer model is known, making self-scoring the natural choice, but unknown-generator scenarios remain a limitation, (11)~all experiments use 256-token segments matching common web-scale chunking; $\\Kempirical$ is defined conditionally on segment length, and longer documents with more stable per-document statistics could yield higher $\\Kempirical$, and (12)~structural composition: depth~0 texts are human-authored (mean ${\\approx}$191 subword tokens after truncation) while depth~1+ are machine-generated (mean 193--256 tokens depending on model; Table~\\ref{tab:token_length}); this length asymmetry makes 0v1 detection \\emph{more conservative} (shorter texts provide less feature-extraction context), not inflated, and depths 1--5 exhibit no systematic length trend (within-model range $\\leq$ 6 tokens; EOS truncation rates stable across depths within each model), so the critical $\\Kempirical{=}2$ finding---based on 1v2 vs.\\ 2v3 comparisons---is unaffected by this confound (Section~\\ref{sec:length_confound}).",
          " Detailed analysis appears in Appendix~\\ref{sec:extended_limitations}.",
          " ",
          " % === Ethics Statement (ACL required, unnumbered) ==="
        ]
      }

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01SfXt1r3BJ18vrdpPCuLmTw`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\subsection{The One-Wash Laundering Effect}
5	\label{sec:why_kstar}
6	\label{sec:one_wash}
7	
8	The shallowness of $\Kempirical = 2$ reflects the concentration of information loss: the 0v1 boundary is near-trivially separable ($\auc \geq 0.96$), 1v2 drops by ${\sim}$30\,pp, and 2v3 falls below $\theta$---the generation operator acts as a lossy channel with diminishing marginal information loss.
9	Consistency across four decoding strategies (Table~\ref{tab:s2_decoding}) reinforces that $\Kempirical$ is governed by the generation process rather than the sampling algorithm.
10	Even at the most marginal 1v2 condition (Qwen-continuation, CI lower bound exceeds $\theta$ by only 0.2\,pp), $\Kempirical = 2$ survives under self-scoring; on the 2v3 side, CI upper bounds confirm non-discrimination in 8/9 conditions, with the single borderline case (nucleus $p{=}0.9$, CI upper${} = 0.601$) consistent with measurement noise at near-threshold values.
11	Cross-scale validation at 7B (Section~\ref{sec:kstar_boundary}) extends this from 1.5B to 7B despite reduced 0v1 signal ($0.9638$ vs.\ $> 0.99$).
12	
13	The practical consequence is a three-tier taxonomy: human-authored (depth~0), first-generation synthetic (depth~1), and deeper synthetic (depth~$\geq 2$).
14	A single generation pass suffices to render text statistically indistinguishable from multiply recycled content---a \emph{one-wash laundering} effect that limits depth-aware corpus curation to a binary boundary.
15	This connects to model collapse: Shumailov et al.~\cite{shumailov2024collapse} observed that tail-distribution degradation is most acute in the earliest generations; our prompt-chain results show an analogous per-text pattern.
16	
17	Per-depth analysis provides the most direct evidence that this boundary is not methodological.
18	OBD achieves 96\% depth-0 recall but only 14--22\% for depths 2--4; frontier LLMs show 69--77\% depth-0 recall and 8--38\% for depths 2--4 (McNemar $p > 0.05$ vs.\ OBD; Table~\ref{tab:s3_llm_zeroshot}).
19	This convergence across fundamentally different approaches---supervised statistical classification and zero-shot frontier LLMs---provides converging evidence that the saturation reflects a data-intrinsic boundary rather than a method limitation.
20	Binary detector confidence corroborates: despite moderate Spearman correlations ($|\rho|$ up to 0.46), 6-class accuracy is at or below chance ($\leq 0.199$ vs.\ 0.167 baseline), and Qwen-rewrite exhibits a sign reversal ($\rho > 0$), disqualifying confidence as a reliable depth proxy.
21	
22	\subsection{Why Graduated Filtering Fails}
23	\label{sec:negative_graduated}
24	
25	Since 2v3 $\auc < \theta$ for all configurations, depth assignments for $d \geq 2$ approximate random labels; graduated weighting injects noise into importance weights, explaining the monotonic improvement with $\alpha$ as it converges toward binary thresholding (Appendix~\ref{sec:extended_limitations}).
26	DSIR fails for a different reason---bigram feature hashing captures domain shift but not depth-correlated distributional signatures.
27	
28	\subsection{Representation Dependence of the Boundary}
29	\label{sec:repr_dependence}
30	
31	$\Kempirical$ is representation-dependent: frozen Qwen embeddings yield $\Kempirical^{\text{neural}} = 3$, while Pythia and OLMo match the 15D boundary at $\Kempirical^{\text{neural}} = 2$ (Table~\ref{tab:neural_probe_crossmodel}).
32	The hierarchy $\Kempirical^{15\text{D}}{=}2 < \Kempirical^{\text{neural}} \in \{2,3\}$ reflects an interpretability--sensitivity tradeoff: learned representations recover additional depth signal sufficient to push $\Kempirical$ to 3 for one model (Qwen), but this gain is model-dependent---the 15D features trade one depth level of sensitivity for cross-model universality (9/9 conditions $\Kempirical{=}2$, 3/3 models identical), making them the preferred operational choice.
33	Cross-scorer validation reinforces this: alternative scorers preserve feature rankings ($\rho > 0.90$) but reduce the marginal Qwen-continuation boundary from $\Kempirical = 2$ to 1, while rewrite-mode $\Kempirical = 2$ is preserved---$\Kempirical$ is governed by the generation operator rather than the scoring model.
34	
35	\subsection{Structural Confound: Token Length and EOS Truncation}
36	\label{sec:length_confound}
37	
38	A potential confound is that EOS truncation at the 256-token generation limit could create systematic length differences across depths, inflating AUC through a length shortcut rather than distributional signatures.
39	We quantify this concern across all model configurations (full per-depth statistics in Table~\ref{tab:token_length}).
40	
41	Depth~0 (human text, truncated from C4) averages ${\sim}$191 tokens across all configurations, while depth~1+ (machine-generated) averages 193--256 tokens depending on model.
42	The length asymmetry thus runs \emph{opposite} to the confound direction: human texts are shorter, so any length-based shortcut would make 0v1 detection \emph{harder} (a classifier exploiting ``shorter$=$machine'' would fail), not easier.
43	
44	In continuation mode, the generation prompt (first half of the predecessor text) is discarded; only the newly generated tokens are retained as the depth-$k$ text.
45	Consequently, all texts at depths $\geq 1$ consist entirely of machine-generated content, and the 1v2 boundary compares texts that differ only in their conditioning context (human-authored vs.\ machine-generated prompt), not in their structural composition.
46	
47	Within machine-generated depths 1--5, token counts are remarkably stable.
48	For Qwen-rewrite, depths 1--5 average 252--254 tokens (range: 2 tokens); for Pythia, 214--218 tokens (range: 4 tokens); for Qwen-7B, 193--197 tokens (range: 4 tokens).
49	This stability means the critical $\Kempirical{=}2$ finding---based on 1v2 vs.\ 2v3 comparisons among machine-generated texts---cannot be driven by length differences, since depths 1 through 5 have effectively identical token counts within each model.
50	
51	OLMo-1B provides the strongest control: it generates exactly 256 tokens at all depths 1--5 with $<$1\% EOS truncation (Table~\ref{tab:token_length}), completely eliminating length variation as a possible confound.
52	$\Kempirical{=}2$ holds for OLMo (1v2 $\auc = 0.615$, 2v3 $\auc = 0.550$; Table~\ref{tab:s8_cv_folds}), demonstrating that the depth boundary arises from distributional rather than length-based signals.
53	
54	EOS truncation rates vary across models (Table~\ref{tab:token_length}): Qwen-rewrite and decoding ablations show mild truncation (7--16\% at depth~1+), Qwen-continuation moderate truncation (17--20\%), while Pythia (34--36\%) and Qwen-7B (45--47\%) exhibit higher rates.
55	Crucially, truncation rates are \emph{stable across depths 1--5 within each model}, so EOS effects do not create a depth-correlated length gradient that could confound pairwise discrimination.
56	The models with highest truncation (Pythia, Qwen-7B) still yield $\Kempirical{=}2$ with the same step-function AUC decay as low-truncation models, further ruling out length as a driver of the boundary.
57	
```
